Continuing work from thread T-019b2280-72a0-71c8-bb79-c045e147d126. When you lack specific information you can use read_thread to get it.

@docs/feature/syntax-highlighting.md @src/theme.rs @themes/dark.yaml @themes/fleet-dark.yaml @themes/github-dark.yaml @themes/github-light.yaml

I just completed the Minimum Viable Product (MVP) for the syntax highlighting feature.

This implementation includes:
- Core data structures for highlights (HighlightToken, LineHighlights, SyntaxHighlights) and language identification (LanguageId).
- An asynchronous background parsing mechanism using a dedicated worker thread and mpsc channels.
- Debouncing of parse requests (50ms timer) and revision-based staleness checks to avoid processing outdated parse results.
- Initial syntax highlighting support for YAML, Markdown, and Rust files using tree-sitter.
- Integration of syntax colors into the theme system via a new SyntaxTheme struct in src/theme.rs, which currently uses hardcoded VS Code-like default colors.
- Rendering of highlighted text in the editor view.
- Automatic triggering of syntax parsing when a document is loaded or its content changes.

I used tree-sitter for parsing, embedding highlight queries for YAML and Markdown, and leveraging tree-sitter-rust's built-in query for Rust.

The docs/feature/syntax-highlighting.md document has been updated with a detailed phased implementation plan, outlining what has been completed (Phase 1A-1E) and what is deferred for future phases, including an "Alignment Checklist (Post-MVP)" for incremental parsing, language injection, and performance optimizations.

The next steps are to update the progress in the roadmap and changelog, and to add the syntax color definitions to the existing builtin theme YAML files.

focus on next steps in the syntax highlighting feature, uipdate progress in roadmap and changelog, add syntax colors to the builtin theme yaml files,

`token --line 42 file.rs` - open file at line 42\n18:   - `token --line 42 --column 10 file.rs` - open at line 42, column 10\n19:   - `token --wait` / `-w` - wait mode for git integration (parsed, not yet implemented)\n20:   - `token ./src` - open directory as workspace (sets workspace_root)\n21: - 1-indexed user input converted to 0-indexed internal representation\n22: - 7 new CLI tests\n23: \n24: ### Added - Duplicate File Detection\n25: \n26: Already-open files now focus existing tab instead of creating duplicates:\n27: \n28: - Overlay disappears when drag leaves window\n42: \n43: ### Changed\n44: \n45: - Test count: 703 (was 603)\n46: - Dependencies: Added `clap = \"4\"` with derive feature\n47: \n48: ---\n49: \n50: ## v0.2.1 - `config_dir()` → `~/.config/token-editor/` (Unix) or `%APPDATA%\\token-editor\\` (Windows)\n58:   - `config_file()` → `config.yaml` path\n59:   - `keymap_file()` → `keymap.yaml` path\n60:   - `themes_dir()` → themes subdirectory\n61:   - `ensure_all_config_dirs()` → creates directory structure\n62: - Respects `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` on Unix if set\n63: - Explicitly uses `~/.config` on macOS (not `~/Library/Application Support`)\n64: \n65: ### Changed - Test Organization\n66: \n67: Moved inline tests to integration test folder:\n68: \n69: - Extracted tests from `config.rs`, `config_paths.rs`, `keymap/defaults.rs`\n70: - New `tests/config.rs` with 22 tests covering config paths, editor config, and keymap merge logic\n71: - Total test count: 597\n72: \n73: ### Fixed - Command Palette Navigation\n74: \n75: - Selection index now properly clamped to filtered item count\n76: - Prevents selecting beyond visible items when filter reduces list\n77: \n78: ### Changed\n79: \n80: - Renamed \"Open Keybindings\" → \"Open Keymap\" in command palette\n81: \n82: ---\n83: \n84: ## 2025-12-15\n85: \n86: ### Added - Configurable Keymapping System\n87: \n88: Complete data-driven keybinding system with YAML configuration:\n89: \n90: **Core Module** (`src/keymap/`):\n91: - `KeyCode`, `Keystroke`, `Modifiers` - platform-agnostic key representation\n92: - `Command` enum - 52 bindable editor commands with `to_msgs()` conversion\n93: - `Keybinding` struct - binds keystroke to command with optional conditions\n94: - `Keymap` - lookup engine with context-aware binding resolution\n95: - `KeyContext` - captures editor state (has_selection, has_multiple_cursors, modal_active, editor_focused)\n96: - `Condition` enum - 7 conditions for context-aware bindings\n97: - YAML parser with platform-specific binding support\n98: \n99: **Default Bindings** (`keymap.yaml`):\n100: - 74 default bindings embedded at compile time\n101: - Platform-aware `cmd` modifier (Cmd on macOS, Ctrl elsewhere)\n102: - macOS-specific bindings (meta+arrow for line navigation)\n103: - Context-aware Tab (indent with selection, insert tab without)\n104: - Context-aware Escape (collapse multi-cursor → clear selection → nothing)\n105: \n106: **Integration** (`src/runtime/app.rs`):\n107: - Keymap tried first for all key events\n108: - Fallback to `input.rs` for complex behaviors (option double-tap, selection collapse on arrows)\n109: - `KeyContext` extracted from model state for binding evaluation\n110: \n111: **Commands Added**:\n112: - `DeleteWordForward` - Option+Delete deletes word after cursor\n113: - `InsertTab` - Insert tab character (for Tab without selection)\n114: - `EscapeSmartClear` - No-op fallback for Escape key\n115: \n116: ### Fixed - Expand Selection Line Behavior\n117: \n118: Fixed line selection to exclude the newline character:\n119: \n120: **Before**: Expanding selection to line selected through the newline, placing cursor at start of next line\n121: **After**: Line selection ends at last character of line content, cursor stays on same line\n122: \n123: - Changed `select_line_at()` to end at last character of line content\n124: - Reordered checks in `expand_selection()` to check `is_line_selection_at` before `is_word_selection_at`\n125: - This fixes single-word lines where word selection equals line selection\n126: - Simplified `is_line_selection_at()` to only check same-line selection ending at line length\n127: \n128: ### Fixed - Option Double-Tap for Multi-Cursor\n129: \n130: Fixed Option double-tap gesture being intercepted by keymap:\n131: \n132: **Before**: Keymap would handle Alt+Up/Down before input.rs could check for double-tap\n133: **After**: Keymap lookup is skipped when `option_double_tapped && alt` is true\n134: \n135: This preserves the Option+Option+Arrow gesture for adding cursors above/below.\n136: \n137: ### Changed\n138: \n139: - Test count: 539 (was 537)\n140: - 66 keymap-specific tests\n141: - 2 new expand selection tests for line behavior\n142: \n143: ---\n144: \n145: ## 2025-12-09\n146: \n147: ### Added - Command Palette (GUI Phase 4)\n148: \n149: Fully functional command palette with searchable command list:\n150: \n151: **Command Registry** (`src/commands.rs`):\n152: - `CommandId` enum with 17 commands (file, edit, navigation, view operations)\n153: - `CommandDef` struct with id, label, keybinding\n154: - `COMMANDS` static registry with all available commands\n155: - `filter_commands(query)` for fuzzy substring matching\n156: \n157: **Command Execution** (`src/update/app.rs`):\n158: - `execute_command(model, cmd_id)` dispatches to appropriate update functions\n159: - Selected item highlighted with selection background\n164: - Keybindings shown right-aligned (dimmed)\n165: - Up/Down arrows navigate list\n166: - Enter executes selected command\n167: - Shows \"... and N more\" when list is truncated\n168: \n169: **Available Commands**:\n170: New File, Save File, Undo, Redo, Cut, Copy, Paste, Select All, Go to Line, Split Editor Right/Down, Close Editor Group, Next/Prev Tab, Close Tab, Find, Show Command Palette\n171: \n172: ---\n173: \n174: ### Added - Mouse Blocking & Compositor (GUI Phase 5)\n175: \n176: Modal overlays now properly capture mouse events:\n177: \n178: **Mouse Blocking** (`src/runtime/app.rs`):\n179: - Click outside modal closes it\n180: - Click inside modal is consumed (doesn't leak to editor)\n181: - Uses centralized `geometry::point_in_modal()` for hit-testing\n182: \n183: **Modal Geometry** (`src/view/geometry.rs`):\n184: - `modal_bounds()` - calculates modal position and size\n185: - `point_in_modal()` - hit-test for modal area\n186: - Shared between rendering and input handling\n187: \n188: ---\n189: \n190: ### Added - Frame Helpers\n191: \n192: New drawing primitives for cleaner rendering:\n193: \n194: - `Frame::draw_bordered_rect()` - fill with 1px border in single call\n195: - Reduces code duplication in modal rendering\n196: \n197: ---\n198: \n199: ### Added - Basic Modal/Focus System (GUI Phase 3)\n200: \n201: Added minimal modal overlay infrastructure with focus capture:\n202: \n203: **Modal State Types** (`src/model/ui.rs`):\n204: - `ModalId` enum: `CommandPalette`, `GotoLine`, `FindReplace`\n205: - `ModalState` enum with per-modal state structs\n206: - `CommandPaletteState`, `GotoLineState`, `FindReplaceState`\n207: - `UiState::active_modal: Option<ModalState>` field\n208: - Helper methods: `has_modal()`, `open_modal()`, `close_modal()`\n209: \n210: **Modal Messages** (`src/messages.rs`):\n211: - `ModalMsg` enum with variants: `Open*`, `Close`, `SetInput`, `InsertChar`, `DeleteBackward`, `SelectPrevious`, `SelectNext`, `Confirm`\n212: - `UiMsg::Modal(ModalMsg)` and `UiMsg::ToggleModal(ModalId)`\n213: \n214: **Focus Capture** (`src/runtime/input.rs`):\n215: - `handle_modal_key()` routes all keyboard input to modal when active\n216: - Modal consumes Escape, Enter, arrows, backspace, and character input\n217: - Editor key handling bypassed when modal is open\n218: \n219: **Modal Rendering** (`src/view/mod.rs`):\n220: - `render_modals()` draws modal overlay layer\n221: - 40% dimmed background over entire window\n222: - Centered modal dialog with title, input field, and blinking cursor\n223: - Rendered after status bar, before debug overlays\n224: \n225: **Keyboard Shortcuts**:\n226: - `Cmd+P` / `Ctrl+P` - Toggle Command Palette\n227: - `Cmd+G` / `Ctrl+G` - Toggle Go to Line\n228: - `Cmd+F` / `Ctrl+F` - Toggle Find/Replace\n229: - `Escape` - Close modal\n230: - `Enter` - Confirm (Go to Line jumps to entered line number)\n231: \n232: #### Benefits\n233: \n234: - Foundation for Command Palette (Phase 4) and other overlays\n235: - Focus capture prevents editor input while modal is open\n236: - Modals are first-class layers with proper z-ordering\n237: - Clean separation of modal state, messages, and rendering\n238: \n239: ---\n240: \n241: ### Added - Widget Extraction & Geometry Centralization (GUI Phase 2)\n242: \n243: Transformed monolithic render function into composable widget functions:\n244: \n245: **New `src/view/geometry.rs` module** - centralized geometry helpers:\n246: - Constants: `TAB_BAR_HEIGHT`, `TABULATOR_WIDTH`\n247: - Viewport sizing: `compute_visible_lines()`, `compute_visible_columns()`\n248: - Tab handling: `expand_tabs_for_display()`, `char_col_to_visual_col()`, `visual_col_to_char_col()`\n249: - Hit-testing: `is_in_status_bar()`, `is_in_tab_bar()`, `tab_at_position()`, `pixel_to_cursor()`\n250: - Layout helpers: `group_content_rect()`, `group_gutter_rect()`, `group_text_area_rect()`\n251: \n252: **Extracted widget renderers:**\n253: - `render_editor_area_static()` - top-level: all groups + splitters\n254: - `render_editor_group_static()` - orchestrates tab bar, gutter, text area\n255: - `render_tab_bar_static()` - tab bar background, tabs, active highlight\n256: - `render_gutter_static()` - line numbers, gutter border\n257: - `render_text_area_static()` - current line highlight, selections, text, cursors\n258: - `render_splitters_static()` - splitter bars between groups\n259: - `render_status_bar_static()` - status bar with segments and separators\n260: \n261: **Updated hit-testing** to delegate to `view::geometry`:\n262: - `Renderer::is_in_status_bar()`, `is_in_tab_bar()`, `tab_at_position()`, `pixel_to_cursor()`\n263: \n264: #### Benefits\n265: \n266: - Prepared for future modal system (Phase 3)\n270: \n271: ---\n272: \n273: ## 2025-12-08\n274: \n275: ### Fixed - Debug Tracing Message Names\n276: \n277: Fixed `msg_type_name()` to show human-readable variant names instead of opaque discriminants:\n278: \n279: **Before:** `msg=Ui::Discriminant(1)`, `msg=Document::Discriminant(0)`  \n280: **After:** `msg=Ui::BlinkCursor`, `msg=Document::InsertChar('a')`\n281: \n282: - `render_perf_overlay()` - fully migrated\n307:   - Status bar rendering - uses Frame/TextPainter\n308: \n309: - **Removed legacy functions**: `draw_text()`, `draw_sparkline()` standalone functions\n310: \n311: #### Benefits\n312: \n313: - `SplitFocused(SplitDirection)` - split current group\n561: - `SplitGroup { group_id, direction }` - split specific group\n562: - `CloseGroup`, `CloseFocusedGroup` - close with layout cleanup\n563: - `FocusGroup`, `FocusNextGroup`, `FocusPrevGroup` - \"package.json\") | |\n ... \n[](https://www.npmjs.com/package/@tree-sitter-grammars/tree-sitter-markdown) [](https://crates.io/crates/tree-sitter-md) [](https://pypi.org/project/tree-sitter-markdown/)\n\nA Markdown parser for [tree-sitter](https://tree-sitter.github.io/tree-sitter/) . [](https://github.com/MDeiml/tree-sitter-markdown/blob/split_parser/.github/screenshot.png)\n\nThe parser is designed to read markdown according to the [CommonMark Spec](https://spec.commonmark.org/) ,\nbut some extensions to the spec from different sources such as [Github flavored markdown](https://github.github.com/gfm/) are also included. These can be toggled on or off at compile time. For specifics see [Extensions]()\n\n## Goals\n\n[]()\n\nEven though this parser has existed for some while and obvious issues are\nmostly solved, there are still lots of inaccuracies in the output. These stem\nfrom restricting a complex format such as markdown to the quite restricting\ntree-sitter parsing rules.\nAs such it is not recommended to use this parser where correctness is\nimportant. The main goal for this parser is to provide syntactical information\nfor syntax highlighting in parsers such as [neovim](https://neovim.io/) and [helix](https://helix-editor.com/) . ## Contributing\n\n[]()\n\nAll contributions are welcome. For details refer to [CONTRIBUTING.md](https://github.com/MDeiml/tree-sitter-markdown/blob/split_parser/CONTRIBUTING.md) . ## Extensions\n\n[]()\n\nExtensions can be enabled at compile time through environment variables. Some\nof them are on by default, these can be disabled with the environment variable `NO_DEFAULT_EXTENSIONS` .\n|Name |Environment variable |Specification |Default |Also enables |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n|Github flavored markdown |`EXTENSION_GFM` |[link](https://github.github.com/gfm/) |✓ |Task lists, strikethrough, pipe tables |\n|Task lists |`EXTENSION_TASK_LIST` |[link](https://github.github.com/gfm/) |✓ | |\n|Strikethrough |`EXTENSION_STRIKETHROUGH` |[link](https://github.github.com/gfm/) |✓ | |\n|Pipe tables |`EXTENSION_PIPE_TABLE` |[link](https://github.github.com/gfm/) |✓ | |\n|YAML metadata |`EXTENSION_MINUS_METADATA` |[link](https://gohugo.io/content-management/front-matter/) |✓ | |\n|TOML metadata |`EXTENSION_PLUS_METADATA` |[link](https://gohugo.io/content-management/front-matter/) |✓ | |\n|Tags |`EXTENSION_TAGS` |[link](https://help.obsidian.md/Editing+and+formatting/Tags+format) | | |\n|Wiki Link |`EXTENSION_WIKI_LINK` |[link](https://help.obsidian.md/Linking+notes+and+files/Internal+links) | | |\n\n## Usage in Editors\n\n[]()\n\nFor guides on how to use this parser in a specific\neditor, refer to that\neditor's specific documentation, e.g. * [neovim](https://github.com/nvim-treesitter/nvim-treesitter)\n* [helix](https://docs.helix-editor.com/guides/adding_languages.html)\n\n## Standalone usage\n\n[]()\n\nTo use the two grammars, first parse the document with the block\ngrammar. Then perform a second parse with the inline grammar using `ts_parser_set_included_ranges` to specify which parts are inline content. These parts are marked as `inline` nodes. Children of those inline nodes should\nbe excluded from these ranges. For an example implementation see `lib.rs` in\nthe `bindings` folder. ### Usage with WASM\n\n[]()\n\nUnfortunately using this parser with WASM/web-tree-sitter does not work out of the box at the moment. This is because the parser uses some C functions that are not exported by tree-sitter by default. To fix this you can statically link the parser to tree-sitter.\nSee also [tree-sitter/tree-sitter](https://github.com/tree-sitter/tree-sitter/issues/949) , [\\](https://github.com/tree-sitter-grammars/tree-sitter-markdown/issues/126) , and [\\](https://github.com/tree-sitter-grammars/tree-sitter-markdown/issues/93)\n\n## About\n\nMarkdown grammar for tree-sitter\n\n### Topics\n\n[markdown](/topics/markdown \"Topic: markdown\") [tree-sitter](/topics/tree-sitter \"Topic: tree-sitter\") [parser](/topics/parser \"Topic: parser\")\n\n### Resources\n\n[Readme]()\n\n### License\n\n[MIT license]()\n\n### Contributing\n\n[Contributing]()\n\n### Uh oh! There was an error while loading. Please reload this page ."

    "url": "https://tree-sitter.github.io/tree-sitter/using-parsers/6-static-node-types",
    "title": "Static Node Types - Tree-sitter",
    "excerpts": [
      "## Keyboard shortcuts\n\nPress `←` or `→` to navigate between chapters\n\nPress `S` or `/` to search in the book\n\nPress `?` to show this help\n\nPress `Esc` to hide this help\n\n* Auto\n* Light\n* Rust\n* Coal\n* Navy\n* Ayu\n\n# Tree-sitter\n\n[](../print.html \"Print this book\") [](https://github.com/tree-sitter/tree-sitter \"Git repository\") [](https://github.com/tree-sitter/tree-sitter/edit/master/docs/src/using-parsers/6-static-node-types.md \"Suggest an edit\")\n\n# [Static Node Types]()\n\nIn languages with static typing, it can be helpful for syntax trees to provide specific type information about individual\nsyntax nodes. Tree-sitter makes this information available via a generated file called `node-types.json` . This _node types_ file provides structured data about every possible syntax node in a grammar.\n\nYou can use this data to generate type declarations in statically-typed programming languages.\n\nThe node types file contains an array of objects, each of which describes a particular type of syntax node using the\nfollowing entries:\n\n## [Basic Info]()\n\nEvery object in this array has these two entries:\n\n* `\"type\"` — A string that indicates, which grammar rule the node represents. This corresponds to the `ts_node_type` function\n  described [here](./2-basic-parsing.html) .\n* `\"named\"` — A boolean that indicates whether this kind of node corresponds to a rule name in the grammar or just a string\n  literal. See [here](./2-basic-parsing.html) for more info.\n\nExamples:\n\n```\n{\n  \"type\": \"string_literal\",\n  \"named\": true\n}\n{\n  \"type\": \"+\",\n  \"named\": false\n}\n```\n\nTogether, these two fields constitute a unique identifier for a node type; no two top-level objects in the `node-types.json` should have the same values for both `\"type\"` and `\"named\"` .\n\n## [Internal Nodes]()\n\nMany syntax nodes can have _children_ . The node type object describes the possible children that a node can have using the\nfollowing entries:\n\n* `\"fields\"` — An object that describes the possible [fields](./2-basic-parsing.html) that the node can have. The keys of this\n  object are field names, and the values are _child type_ objects, described below.\n* `\"children\"` — Another _child type_ object that describes all the node's possible _named_ children _without_ fields.\n\nA _child type_ object describes a set of child nodes using the following entries:\n\n* `\"required\"` — A boolean indicating whether there is always _at least one_ node in this set.\n* `\"multiple\"` — A boolean indicating whether there can be _multiple_ nodes in this set.\n* `\"types\"` \\- An array of objects that represent the possible types of nodes in this set. Each object has two keys: `\"type\"` and `\"named\"` , whose meanings are described above.\n\nExample with fields:\n\n```\n{\n  \"type\": \"method_definition\",\n  \"named\": true,\n  \"fields\": {\n    \"body\": {\n      \"multiple\": false,\n      \"required\": true,\n      \"types\": [{ \"type\": \"statement_block\", \"named\": true }]\n    },\n    \"decorator\": {\n      \"multiple\": true,\n      \"required\": false,\n      \"types\": [{ \"type\": \"decorator\", \"named\": true }]\n    },\n    \"name\": {\n      \"multiple\": false,\n      \"required\": true,\n      \"types\": [\n        { \"type\": \"computed_property_name\", \"named\": true },\n        { \"type\": \"property_identifier\", \"named\": true }\n      ]\n    },\n    \"parameters\": {\n      \"multiple\": false,\n      \"required\": true,\n      \"types\": [{ \"type\": \"formal_parameters\", \"named\": true }]\n    }\n  }\n}\n```\n\nExample with children:\n\n```\n{\n  \"type\": \"array\",\n  \"named\": true,\n  \"fields\": {},\n  \"children\": {\n    \"multiple\": true,\n    \"required\": false,\n    \"types\": [\n      { \"type\": \"_expression\", \"named\": true },\n      { \"type\": \"spread_element\", \"named\": true }\n    ]\n  }\n}\n```\n\n## [Supertype Nodes]()\n\nIn Tree-sitter grammars, there are usually certain rules that represent abstract _categories_ of syntax nodes (e.g. \"expression\",\n\"type\", \"declaration\"). In the `grammar.js` file, these are often written as [hidden rules](../creating-parsers/3-writing-the-grammar.html) whose definition is a simple [`choice`](../creating-parsers/2-the-grammar-dsl.html) where each member is just a single symbol.\n\nNormally, hidden rules are not mentioned in the node types file, since they don't appear in the syntax tree. But if you add\na hidden rule to the grammar's [`supertypes` list](../creating-parsers/2-the-grammar-dsl.html) , then it _will_ show up in the node\ntypes file, with the following special entry:\n\n* `\"subtypes\"` — An array of objects that specify the _types_ of nodes that this 'supertype' node can wrap.\n\nExample:\n\n```\n{\n  \"type\": \"_declaration\",\n  \"named\": true,\n  \"subtypes\": [\n    { \"type\": \"class_declaration\", \"named\": true },\n    { \"type\": \"function_declaration\", \"named\": true },\n    { \"type\": \"generator_function_declaration\", \"named\": true },\n    { \"type\": \"lexical_declaration\", \"named\": true },\n    { \"type\": \"variable_declaration\", \"named\": true }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\nSupertype nodes will also appear elsewhere in the node types file, as children of other node types, in a way that corresponds\nwith how the supertype rule was used in the grammar. This can make the node types much shorter and easier to read, because\na single supertype will take the place of multiple subtypes.\n\nExample:\n\n```\n{\n  \"type\": \"export_statement\",\n  \"named\": true,\n  \"fields\": {\n    \"declaration\": {\n      \"multiple\": false,\n      \"required\": false,\n      \"types\": [{ \"type\": \"_declaration\", \"named\": true }]\n    },\n    \"source\": {\n      \"multiple\": false,\n      \"required\": false,\n      \"types\": [{ \"type\": \"string\", \"named\": true }]\n    }\n  }\n}\n```\n\n[](../using-parsers/queries/4-api.html \"Previous chapter\") [](../using-parsers/7-abi-versions.html \"Next chapter\")\n\n[](../using-parsers/queries/4-api.html \"Previous chapter\") [](../using-parsers/7-abi-versions.html \"Next chapter\")\n"
    ]
  },

      "[Skip to main content]()\n\nBack to top\n\n`Ctrl` \\+ `K`\n\n[](https://github.com/tree-sitter/py-tree-sitter \"Source repository\")\n\n# Node [\\#]( \"Link to this heading\")\n\n_class_ tree\\_sitter. Node [\\#](.Node \"Link to this definition\")\n    A single node within a syntax `Tree` .\n\n## Methods [\\#]( \"Link to this heading\")\n\nchild ( _index_ , _/_ ) [\\#](.Node.child \"Link to this definition\")\n    Get this node’s child at the given index, where `0` represents the first child.\n\nCaution\n\nThis method is fairly fast, but its cost is technically `log(i)` , so if you might be iterating over a long list of children, you should use [`children`](.Node.children \"tree_sitter.Node.children\") or [`walk()`](.Node.walk \"tree_sitter.Node.walk\") instead.\n\nchild\\_by\\_field\\_id ( _id_ , _/_ ) [\\#](.Node.child_by_field_id \"Link to this definition\")\n    Get the first child with the given numerical field id.\n\nHint\n\nYou can convert a field name to an id using [`Language.field_id_for_name()`](tree_sitter.Language.html.Language.field_id_for_name \"tree_sitter.Language.field_id_for_name\") .\n\nSee also\n\n[`child_by_field_name()`](.Node.child_by_field_name \"tree_sitter.Node.child_by_field_name\")\n\nchild\\_by\\_field\\_name ( _name_ , _/_ ) [\\#](.Node.child_by_field_name \"Link to this definition\")\n    Get the first child with the given field name.\n\nchild\\_with\\_descendant ( _descendant_ , _/_ ) [\\#](.Node.child_with_descendant \"Link to this definition\")\n    Get the node that contains the given descendant.\n\nchildren\\_by\\_field\\_id ( _id_ , _/_ ) [\\#](.Node.children_by_field_id \"Link to this definition\")\n    Get a list of children with the given numerical field id.\n\nSee also\n\n[`children_by_field_name()`](.Node.children_by_field_name \"tree_sitter.Node.children_by_field_name\")\n\nchildren\\_by\\_field\\_name ( _name_ , _/_ ) [\\#](.Node.children_by_field_name \"Link to this definition\")\n    Get a list of children with the given field name.\n\ndescendant\\_for\\_byte\\_range ( _start\\_byte_ , _end\\_byte_ , _/_ ) [\\#](.Node.descendant_for_byte_range \"Link to this definition\")\n    Get the smallest node within this node that spans the given byte range.\n\ndescendant\\_for\\_point\\_range ( _start\\_point_ , _end\\_point_ , _/_ ) [\\#](.Node.descendant_for_point_range \"Link to this definition\")\n    Get the smallest node within this node that spans the given point range.\n\nedit ( _start\\_byte_ , _old\\_end\\_byte_ , _new\\_end\\_byte_ , _start\\_point_ , _old\\_end\\_point_ , _new\\_end\\_point_ ) [\\#](.Node.edit \"Link to this definition\")\n    Edit this node to keep it in-sync with source code that has been edited.\n\nNote\n\nThis method is only rarely needed. When you edit a syntax tree via [`Tree.edit()`](tree_sitter.Tree.html.Tree.edit \"tree_sitter.Tree.edit\") , all of the nodes that you retrieve from the tree afterwards will already reflect the edit. You only need to use this when you have a specific [`Node`](.Node \"tree_sitter.Node\") instance that you want to keep and continue to use after an edit.\n\nfield\\_name\\_for\\_child ( _child\\_index_ , _/_ ) [\\#](.Node.field_name_for_child \"Link to this definition\")\n    Get the field name of this node’s child at the given index.\n\nfield\\_name\\_for\\_named\\_child ( ) [\\#](.Node.field_name_for_named_child \"Link to this definition\")\n    field\\_name\\_for\\_child(self, child\\_index, /)\n–\n\nGet the field name of this node’s _named_ child at the given index.\n\nfirst\\_child\\_for\\_byte ( _byte_ , _/_ ) [\\#](.Node.first_child_for_byte \"Link to this definition\")\n    Get the node’s first child that contains or starts after the given byte offset.\n\nAdded in version 0.25.0.\n\nfirst\\_named\\_child\\_for\\_byte ( _byte_ , _/_ ) [\\#](.Node.first_named_child_for_byte \"Link to this definition\")\n    Get the node’s first _named_ child that contains or starts after the given byte offset.\n\nAdded in version 0.25.0.\n\nnamed\\_child ( _index_ , _/_ ) [\\#](.Node.named_child \"Link to this definition\")\n    Get this node’s _named_ child at the given index, where `0` represents the first child.\n\nCaution\n\nThis method is fairly fast, but its cost is technically `log(i)` , so if you might be iterating over a long list of children, you should use [`children`](.Node.children \"tree_sitter.Node.children\") or [`walk()`](.Node.walk \"tree_sitter.Node.walk\") instead.\n\nnamed\\_descendant\\_for\\_byte\\_range ( _start\\_byte_ , _end\\_byte_ , _/_ ) [\\#](.Node.named_descendant_for_byte_range \"Link to this definition\")\n    Get the smallest _named_ node within this node that spans the given byte range.\n\nnamed\\_descendant\\_for\\_point\\_range ( _start\\_point_ , _end\\_point_ , _/_ ) [\\#](.Node.named_descendant_for_point_range \"Link to this definition\")\n    Get the smallest _named_ node within this node that spans the given point range.\n\nwalk ( ) [\\#](.Node.walk \"Link to this definition\")\n    Create a new [`TreeCursor`](tree_sitter.TreeCursor.html.TreeCursor \"tree_sitter.TreeCursor\") starting from this node.\n\n## Special Methods [\\#]( \"Link to this heading\")\n\n\\_\\_eq\\_\\_ ( _value_ , _/_ ) [\\#](.Node.__eq__ \"Link to this definition\")\n    Implements `self==value` .\n\n\\_\\_hash\\_\\_ ( ) [\\#](.Node.__hash__ \"Link to this definition\")\n    Implements `hash(self)` .\n\n\\_\\_ne\\_\\_ ( _value_ , _/_ ) [\\#](.Node.__ne__ \"Link to \"Link to this definition\")\n    Implements `str(self)` .\n\n## Attributes [\\#]( \"Link to this heading\")\n\nbyte\\_range [\\#](.Node.byte_range \"Link to this definition\")\n    The byte range of source code that this node represents, in terms of bytes.\n\nchild\\_count [\\#](.Node.child_count \"Link to this definition\")\n    This node’s number of children.\n\nchildren [\\#](.Node.children \"Link to this definition\")\n    This node’s children.\n\nNote\n\nIf you’re walking the tree recursively, you may want to use [`walk()`](.Node.walk \"tree_sitter.Node.walk\") instead.\n\ndescendant\\_count [\\#](.Node.descendant_count \"Link to this definition\")\n    This node’s number of descendants, including the node itself.\n\nend\\_byte [\\#](.Node.end_byte \"Link to this definition\")\n    The byte offset where this node ends.\n\nend\\_point [\\#](.Node.end_point \"Link to this definition\")\n    This node’s end point.\n\ngrammar\\_id [\\#](.Node.grammar_id \"Link to this definition\")\n    This node’s type as a numerical id as it appears in the grammar ignoring aliases.\n\ngrammar\\_name [\\#](.Node.grammar_name \"Link to this definition\")\n    This node’s symbol name as it appears in the grammar ignoring aliases.\n\nhas\\_changes [\\#](.Node.has_changes \"Link to this definition\")\n    Check if this node has been edited.\n\nhas\\_error [\\#](.Node.has_error \"Link to this definition\")\n    Check if this node represents a syntax error or contains any syntax errors anywhere within it.\n\nid [\\#](.Node.id \"Link to this definition\")\n    This node’s numerical id.\n\nNote\n\nWithin a given syntax tree, no two nodes have the same id. However, if a new tree is created based on an older tree, and a node from the old tree is reused in the process, then that node will have the same id in both trees.\n\nis\\_error [\\#](.Node.is_error \"Link to this definition\")\n    Check if this node represents a syntax error.\n\nSyntax errors represent parts of the code that could not be incorporated into a valid syntax tree.\n\nis\\_extra [\\#](.Node.is_extra \"Link to this definition\")\n    Check if this node is \\_extra\\_.\n\nExtra nodes represent things which are not required by the grammar but can appear anywhere (e.g. whitespace).\n\nis\\_missing [\\#](.Node.is_missing \"Link to this definition\")\n    Check if this node is \\_missing\\_.\n\nMissing nodes are inserted by the parser in order to recover from certain kinds of syntax errors.\n\nis\\_named [\\#](.Node.is_named \"Link to this definition\")\n    Check if this node is \\_named\\_.\n\nNamed nodes correspond to named rules in the grammar, whereas _anonymous_ nodes correspond to string literals in the grammar.\n\nkind\\_id [\\#](.Node.kind_id \"Link to this definition\")\n    This node’s type as a numerical id.\n\nnamed\\_child\\_count [\\#](.Node.named_child_count \"Link to this definition\")\n    This node’s number of \\_named\\_ children.\n\nnamed\\_children [\\#](.Node.named_children \"Link to this definition\")\n    This node’s \\_named\\_ children.\n\nnext\\_named\\_sibling [\\#](.Node.next_named_sibling \"Link to this definition\")\n    This node’s next named sibling.\n\nnext\\_parse\\_state [\\#](.Node.next_parse_state \"Link to this definition\")\n    The parse state after this node.\n\nnext\\_sibling [\\#](.Node.next_sibling \"Link to this definition\")\n    This node’s next sibling.\n\nparent [\\#](.Node.parent \"Link to this definition\")\n    This node’s immediate parent.\n\nparse\\_state [\\#](.Node.parse_state \"Link to this definition\")\n    This node’s parse state.\n\nprev\\_named\\_sibling [\\#](.Node.prev_named_sibling \"Link to this definition\")\n    This node’s previous named sibling.\n\nprev\\_sibling [\\#](.Node.prev_sibling \"Link to this definition\")\n    This node’s previous sibling.\n\nrange [\\#](.Node.range \"Link to this definition\")\n    The range of source code that this node represents.\n\nstart\\_byte [\\#](.Node.start_byte \"Link to this definition\")\n    The   {
    "url": "https://slar.se/syntax-highlight-anything-with-tree-sitter.html",
    "title": "Syntax highlight anything with Tree-sitter",
    "excerpts": [
      "Toggle navigation [](https://slar.se/)\n\n* [About](https://slar.se/pages/about.html)\n* [Publications and Essays](https://slar.se/pages/publications-and-essays.html)\n* [Wiki](https://slar.se/pages/wiki.html)\n* [License](https://slar.se/pages/license.html)\n* [Projects](https://slar.se/pages/projects.html)\n* [Resume](https://slar.se/resume.pdf)\n* [Archives](https://slar.se/archives.html)\n* [](https://slar.se/feeds/all.atom.xml)\n\n# [Programming for fun and profit](https://slar.se/)\n\nA blog about software engineering, programming languages and technical tinkering\n\nMon 11 March 2024\n\n# [Syntax highlight anything with Tree-sitter](https://slar.se/syntax-highlight-anything-with-tree-sitter.html \"Permalink to Syntax highlight anything with Tree-sitter\")\n\nPosted by [Simon Larsén](https://slar.se/author/simon-larsen.html) in [Programming](https://slar.se/category/programming.html)\n\nTags [tree-sitter](https://slar.se/tag/tree-sitter.html) [parsing](https://slar.se/tag/parsing.html)\n[syntax](https://slar.se/tag/syntax.html) [neovim](https://slar.se/tag/neovim.html)\n\nAs of my previous post on [extending NeoVim for commenting and uncommenting code blocks](https://slar.se/comment-and-uncomment-code-in-neovim.html) , I'm on something of a\nNeoVim extension streak. The flavor of the week is syntax highlighting. I've been\nusing the highly customizable [nvim-treesitter](https://github.com/nvim-treesitter/nvim-treesitter) for the\npast few years. This depends on there being a [Tree-sitter](https://tree-sitter.github.io/tree-sitter/) parser for whatever\nlanguage you're working with. Usually there is, but sometimes you run into\nlanguages that are esoteric enough that there aren't any parsers available. And\nthen you're SOL on the whole syntax highlighting part. Unless, of course, you\nwrite a parser of your own. Which naturally is what we'll do. This is the first article in a series on working with Tree-sitter to syntax\nhighlight anything.\nAlthough this article series is intended for a\nNeoVim-inclined audience, this first part has nothing to do with NeoVim and can\nbe enjoyed by anyone interested in Tree-sitter or parsers in general. In the\nstill under construction second part, we'll dive into working with and utilizing\nTree-sitter in NeoVim. > **Companion repository:** The complete parser developed in this article is\n> available in a companion repository at [slarse/tree-sitter-mds](https://github.com/slarse/tree-sitter-mds)\n> \n> \n\n# What's this Tree-sitter thing? [Tree-sitter](https://tree-sitter.github.io/tree-sitter/) is a parser generator\ntool for the modern era. What's a parser generator, you ask? Well, it's exactly\nwhat it sounds like: a tool to generate a parser! By defining the syntax of a\nlanguage in a way the parser generator understands, it can generate a parser\nfor you that can take source code of that language and transform it into a\nsyntax tree. Parser generators aren't a new concept.\n ... \n> \n> \n\n# Working example: Markdown Simple\n\nAs a driving example for this article series, we'll look at a tiny subset of\nMarkdown, which should be familiar to most anyone in the target audience. Specifically, we want to be able to highlight the following containing\nheadings, paragraphs, inline code and code blocks. ```\n# This is a heading This little paragraph of text with ` inline code ` ``` const words = [ \"javascript\" , \"code\" , \"highlighting\" ]; for ( const word of words ) { console . log ( word ); } ``` # This is another heading Another paragraph with a # in the middle. ```\n\n**Store these sample file contents in `test.mds` for later use** ! I call this subset _Markdown Simple_ and choose to write it in files with the `.mds` file extension. At the end of this article, we'll have fully functioning\nsyntax highlighting for `.mds` files, including properly highlighted JavaScript\ncode in the multiline code blocks!\n ... \n```\n$ npx tree-sitter generate\n$ npx tree-sitter parse test.mds\n```\n\nThis should produce a simple syntax tree. ```\n( source_file [ 0, 0 ] - [ 13, 0 ] ( text [ 0, 0 ] - [ 13, 0 ]))\n```\n\nLet's dissect the grammar in detail so we can improve the syntax tree\ngranularity. The root node is the `source_file` . Every syntax tree is rooted in\nthis node, and there is only one of them for each parsed file. The rule\ndefinition may look a bit strange at first sight. ```\nsource_file : $ => $ . text\n```\n\nAny non-trivial grammar is built by composing rules. Any rule that references\nanother rule is a so-called [_non-terminal_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terminal_and_nonterminal_symbols) . The above rule says that the `source_file` consists of precisely one `text` node. So `source_file` is a non-terminal that references `text` . We then define the `text` rule using regex. ```\n_ => / (. +| \\ n ) / ,\n```\n\nThis regex just captures any character ( `.` ) or line feeds ( `\\n` ).\n ... \n> \n> \n\n```\n\"tree-sitter\" : [ { \"scope\" : \"source.markdownsimple\" , \"file-types\" : [ \"mds\" ], \"highlights\" : [ \"queries/highlights.scm\" ] } ],\n```\n\nNow run the `highlight` command again. If you get the _same_ error as before,\nyou need to add the directory that contains you project's directory to\nTree-sitter's `config.json` file. For example, my project is located in `/home/slarse/projects/tree-sitter/tree-sitter-mds` , so I add the path `/home/slarse/projects/tree-sitter` to the list of paths at the top of `/home/slarse/.config/tree-sitter/config.json` . With that fixed, you should get a new error. ```\n$ npx tree-sitter highlight test.mds\nFailed to read query file \"queries/highlights.scm\" \n\nCaused by:\n No such file or directory ( os error 2 )\n```\n\nThis means your project was correctly located. We now need to define _queries_ for our highlight. Queries allow us to select nodes in the syntax tree and\nassign pre-defined semantic meaning to them.\nPut the following content in the\nfile `queries/highlights.scm` in your project directory. ```\n( text ) @string\n```\n\n> I won't go into detail on queries in this article. You can find all captures\n> (e.g. `@string` ) available [over here](https://github.com/nvim-treesitter/nvim-treesitter/blob/master/CONTRIBUTING.md) ,\n> and you can read more about queries as a whole [over here](https://tree-sitter.github.io/tree-sitter/using-parsers) . > \n> \n\nThis says that any `text` node should be considered a `string` . Running the\nhighlight command again, you ought to get some colored output. Not all that impressive; as all nodes with any content are `text` nodes\neverything is just highlighted as a string. In my Tree-sitter configuration\nthe `string` color happens happens to be a shade of green, but it may be\ndifferent for you. For more granular highlighting, we need a more granular\nsyntax tree to perform queries against. # Refining the grammar\n\nThe basic pieces of the puzzle are now in place.\nWe have a rudimentary syntax\ntree allowing for an equally rudimentary syntax highlighting. It's now time to\nstart refining the grammar to capture different components. With some crude\nannotations in `()` , we want a structure like this. ```\n( section ) # This is a heading (heading) This little paragraph of text with ` inline code ` ( paragraph ( text ) ( inlineCode ( codeText ))) ``` ( codeBlock ( codeText )) const words = [ \"javascript\" , \"code\" , \"highlighting\" ]; for ( const word of words ) { console . log ( word ); } ``` # This is another heading (heading) And this is another little paragraph . ( paragraph ( text ))\n```\n\nLet's start off only with capturing the additional `section` and `heading` nodes\nwith new rules. ```\nrules : { source_file : $ => repeat ( $ . section ), section : $ => seq ( $ . heading , repeat ( $ . text ), ), heading : _ => / # . + /, text : _ => / (.\n ... \n```\ntext : _ => / ([ ^ # ] | [ ^ \\ n ] # ) + /,\n```\n\nHere we say that `text` is either not a `#` , or it's a non-linefeed character\nfollowed by `#` . Generating and parsing again we should now have our basic\nsection structure mapped out. ```\n( source_file [ 0, 0 ] - [ 13, 0 ] ( section [ 0, 0 ] - [ 11, 0 ] ( heading [ 0, 0 ] - [ 0, 19 ]) ( text [ 0, 19 ] - [ 11, 0 ])) ( section [ 11, 0 ] - [ 13, 0 ] ( heading [ 11, 0 ] - [ 11, 25 ]) ( text [ 11, 25 ] - [ 13, 0 ])))\n```\n\nGreat! We can now also adjust the query in `queries/highlights.scm` to only\nhighlight headings. ```\n( heading ) @string\n```\n\nThis should land you with the following highlighting. Nice! # Capturing inline code and code blocks\n\nThe only thing left for us to capture is inline code and code blocks. This is\nhowever where it starts to get a little bit complicated, so let's take each of\nthese in turn. ## Capturing code blocks\n\nCode blocks are relatively simple to capture in isolation.\nA rule for code\nblocks could look as follows. ```\ncodeBlock : $ => seq ( '```' , $ . codeText , '```' , ), codeText : _ => / [ ^ `]*/,\n```\n\nWe could inline `codeText` into the `codeBlock` , but making it a separate rule\nwill make it easier for us to highlight the content of the code block without\naffecting the backticks around it. Note that `codeText` is not quite the same as `text` ; the former allows any _zero or more_ characters except for backticks. This will of course make it impossible to use backticks inside of a `codeBlock` ,\nwhich is a simplification we're going with here. Extending the rule to allow\nfor backticks within a `codeBlock` node is left as an exercise to the reader. We can now extend our `section` rule to contain `codeBlock` s.\n\n```\nsection : $ => seq ( $ . heading , repeat ( choice ( $ . text , $ . codeBlock , ) ), ),\n```\n\nIf you put this in and run it, you may be a bit surprised to see that the output\ndoesn't change from before.\nThe syntax tree still consists of two `section` s\ncontaining one `heading` and `text` each. This is because `text` is consuming\nthe backticks that should delimit a `codeBlock` . We'll address this at the same\ntime as we define our paragraphs in the next part. ## Paragraphs with text and inline code\n\nWe want to define grammar rules such that the parser can recognize _paragraphs_ that are composed of one or more `text` and/or `inlineCode` nodes. The `inlineCode` rule is quite simple: a backtick, followed by zero or more\ncharacters that are anything but a backtick (i.e. `codeText` ), followed by a\nbacktick. ```\ninlineCode : $ => seq ( '`' , $ . codeText , '`' , ),\n```\n\nWe also need to alter the `text` terminal to not allow backticks, because\notherwise it will just consume them like it did the `#` before. This will also\nallow our `codeBlock` rule to actually consume something.\n ... \ndelimiter ( codeBlock ( codeText ) @module )\n```\n\nHere we have our first non-trivial use of queries, as we have two queries\nreferencing the same type of node: `codeBlock` . The first query marks the\nentire `codeBlock` with `@punctuation.delimiter` , which is dark grey in my color\nscheme. Then we mark `codeText` _inside_ the `codeBlock` as a `@module` , which\nis yellow in my color scheme. The latter query is more specific than the former,\nand so takes precedence. The result is a significant improvement over our\nstarting point. But it's still not _great_ . Having yellow JavaScript code is a marginal\nimprovement over just white code. This is where Tree-sitter's killer syntax\nhighlighting feature comes into play: [injections](https://tree-sitter.github.io/tree-sitter/syntax-highlighting)\n\n## Injecting a JavaScript parser\n\nInjections are super cool. They allow us to succinctly state that certain nodes\nshould be parsed with some other parser than the one we're currently using."
    ]
  }
]
```

I see - we're using `tree_sitter_md::LANGUAGE` which is the **block** grammar. The markdown grammar from tree-sitter-md has two parts - block and inline. The `code_span` node exists only in the inline grammar, not in the block grammar.

The simplest fix is to remove the `code_span` line and use only nodes that exist in the block grammar, or use tree-sitter-md's built-in queries. Let me simplify the markdown query to only use valid block-level node types:

## Summary

I found where syntax highlights are rendered in the editor view. Here are the key files:

**Main Rendering Files:**

1. **`~/code/rust-editor/src/view/frame.rs`** - Contains the `TextPainter::draw_with_highlights()` function (lines 308-380+) that handles the actual rendering of text with syntax highlighting colors. This function:
   - Iterates through each character in the text
   - Maps each character to its corresponding syntax highlight token
   - Looks up the color for that highlight using `syntax_theme.color_for_highlight()`
   - Rasterizes and blits each character with the appropriate color to the frame buffer
   - Performs alpha blending for anti-aliasing

2. **`~/code/rust-editor/src/view/mod.rs`** - Contains the main view rendering logic (lines 530-591) that:
   - Retrieves line highlights using `document.get_line_highlights(doc_line)` (line 556)
   - Adjusts token columns for viewport scroll and tab expansion (lines 561-576)
   - Calls `painter.draw_with_highlights()` when there are syntax tokens to apply (lines 581-589)
   - Falls back to plain `painter.draw()` when there are no highlights (line 579)

**Related Structure Files:**
- `src/syntax/highlights.rs` - Defines the `SyntaxHighlights` data structure and `HighlightToken`
- `src/view/geometry.rs` - Contains geometry utilities for the view

The syntax highlighting rendering flow works by: Document → get_line_highlights() → adjust tokens for viewport → draw_with_highlights() → per-character color lookup → frame buffer blitting.

Perfect! Now I have all the pieces. Let me create a comprehensive summary document with all the relevant files:

Based on my analysis of the codebase, here are the files that handle `Cmd::ParseSyntax` dispatch and processing, along with how syntax parsing is triggered when documents are opened or edited:

## Core Files for Syntax Parsing

### 1. **Command Definition**
- `~/code/rust-editor/src/commands.rs` - Defines the `Cmd` enum with:
  - `Cmd::DebouncedSyntaxParse { document_id, revision, delay_ms }` - Schedules a debounced parse
  - `Cmd::RunSyntaxParse { document_id, revision, source, language }` - Runs actual parsing

### 2. **Message Types**
- `~/code/rust-editor/src/messages.rs` - Defines `SyntaxMsg` enum:
  - `SyntaxMsg::ParseReady { document_id, revision }` - Fired after debounce timer
  - `SyntaxMsg::ParseCompleted { document_id, revision, highlights }` - Fired when parsing completes
  - `SyntaxMsg::LanguageChanged { document_id, language }` - Fired when language changes

### 3. **Update Handlers**
- `~/code/rust-editor/src/update/syntax.rs` - Main syntax update logic:
  - `update_syntax()` - Handles `SyntaxMsg` events and dispatches `Cmd::RunSyntaxParse` or `Cmd::DebouncedSyntaxParse`
  - `schedule_syntax_parse()` - Public helper to create a debounced parse command

- `~/code/rust-editor/src/update/app.rs` - Triggers parsing when files are loaded:
  - `AppMsg::FileLoaded` handler (lines 66-106) - After a file is loaded, returns `Cmd::DebouncedSyntaxParse` if language has highlighting

- `~/code/rust-editor/src/update/layout.rs` - Document opening:
  - `open_file_in_new_tab()` - Opens files, creating documents that can have syntax parsed

- `~/code/rust-editor/src/update/document.rs` - Document editing:
  - Each edit operation (InsertChar, InsertNewline, DeleteBackward, etc.) marks document as modified and increments revision via `push_edit()`

### 4. **Command Execution**
- `~/code/rust-editor/src/runtime/app.rs` - Executes commands:
  - Lines 775-791: `Cmd::DebouncedSyntaxParse` handler - Spawns a timer thread that sends `SyntaxMsg::ParseReady` after delay
  - Lines 793-806: `Cmd::RunSyntaxParse` handler - Sends request to syntax worker thread
  - `syntax_worker_loop()` (lines 897-926) - Background thread that parses and sends results back as `SyntaxMsg::ParseCompleted`

### 5. **Syntax Module**
- `~/code/rust-editor/src/syntax/mod.rs` - Defines the syntax highlighting architecture
- `~/code/rust-editor/src/syntax/parser.rs` - Contains `ParserState` for tree-sitter parsing

## How Syntax Parsing is Triggered

### When a Document is Opened:
1. File is opened via `LayoutMsg::OpenFileInNewTab` → `open_file_in_new_tab()`
2. Document is loaded with language auto-detected from file extension
3. `AppMsg::FileLoaded` handler checks if language has highlighting support
4. If yes, returns `Cmd::DebouncedSyntaxParse` with 50ms delay

### When a Document is Edited:
1. User types or edits text → Document message (e.g., `DocumentMsg::InsertChar`)
2. `update_document()` processes the edit and calls `doc.push_edit()`
3. `push_edit()` increments document `revision` counter (line 215 in `~/code/rust-editor/src/model/document.rs`)
4. The edit operation marks document as `is_modified = true`
5. **Note:** The actual syntax parse triggering on edits appears to be deferred - the document tracks changes via the revision counter, but a separate mechanism may trigger parsing after edits complete

### Processing Pipeline:
```
Document Edit → push_edit() → revision++, is_modified=true
                           → Cmd::Redraw returned

OR (when file opened):
FileLoaded → Cmd::DebouncedSyntaxParse (50ms)
          → SyntaxMsg::ParseReady (after timer)
          → Cmd::RunSyntaxParse
          → (worker thread parses)
          → SyntaxMsg::ParseCompleted
          → Cmd::Redraw
```

## Assistant

Now let me provide a summary of what was fixed:

**Problem:** Syntax highlighting wasn't being displayed because:

1. **No initial parsing trigger:** Documents loaded at startup via CLI arguments bypassed the `FileLoaded` message handler that triggers syntax parsing. They were loaded synchronously in `create_initial_session()`.

2. **No parsing on edits:** Document edit operations (InsertChar, InsertNewline, DeleteBackward, etc.) returned `Cmd::Redraw` but didn't schedule syntax re-parsing.

**Fixes applied:**

1. **Added `trigger_initial_syntax_parsing()`** in [src/runtime/app.rs](file://~/code/rust-editor/src/runtime/app.rs#L127-L145) - Called after `App::new()` to send parse requests to the syntax worker for all documents with highlighting support.

2. **Added `redraw_with_syntax_parse()` helper** in [src/update/document.rs](file://~/code/rust-editor/src/update/document.rs#L13-L21) - Batches `Cmd::Redraw` with `Cmd::DebouncedSyntaxParse` for the current document.

3. **Updated all document edit handlers** to use `redraw_with_syntax_parse(model)` instead of `Cmd::Redraw` - This ensures syntax re-parsing (with 50ms debounce) after every text change.

Try opening a Rust, YAML, or Markdown file now and you should see syntax highlighting.

## User

**Tool Result:** `toolu_01V2FW6nFqfA6PgcyJGKgd6B`

```
1) TL;DR

- Bug 1 (only one tab highlighted): the syntax worker uses a “latest request wins” loop that drops all but the last parse request across *all* documents, so only one document ever gets highlights.
- Bug 2 (wonky after edit): tree‑sitter’s `column` is a byte offset, but you treat it as a character index (and also ignore tab expansion), so highlight spans don’t line up with rendered text, especially after edits and with non‑ASCII or tabs.

---

2) Recommended approach (simple path)

### A. Fix “only first document gets syntax highlighting”

**Where the bug is**

In `runtime/app.rs`:

```rust
fn syntax_worker_loop(rx: Receiver<SyntaxParseRequest>, msg_tx: Sender<Msg>) {
    let mut parser_state = ParserState::new();

    while let Ok(mut req) = rx.recv() {
        // Drain any queued requests, only keep the latest
        while let Ok(next) = rx.try_recv() {
            req = next;
        }

        // Skip plain text (no highlighting)
        if !req.language.has_highlighting() {
            continue;
        }

        // Parse and extract highlights
        let highlights = parser_state.parse_and_highlight(
            &req.source,
            req.language,
            req.document_id,
            req.revision,
        );

        // Send results back to main thread
        let _ = msg_tx.send(Msg::Syntax(SyntaxMsg::ParseCompleted {
            document_id: req.document_id,
            revision: req.revision,
            highlights,
        }));
    }
}
```

That inner `while let Ok(next) = rx.try_recv()` drains the entire queue and keeps only the *last* request, regardless of which document it belongs to.

- On startup (`trigger_initial_syntax_parsing`), you send one `SyntaxParseRequest` per open document.
- The worker immediately drains them and only processes the last one.
- Only that document ever gets `ParseCompleted` → `doc.syntax_highlights = Some(highlights)`.
- All other documents’ `syntax_highlights` stay `None`, so `render_text_area` sees no tokens and draws plain text.

**Minimal fix**

Remove the global “latest request wins” behavior and process each request sequentially; you already debounce on the main thread, so you don’t need this extra collapsing.

Replace `syntax_worker_loop` with:

```rust
fn syntax_worker_loop(rx: Receiver<SyntaxParseRequest>, msg_tx: Sender<Msg>) {
    let mut parser_state = ParserState::new();

    while let Ok(req) = rx.recv() {
        // Skip plain text (no highlighting)
        if !req.language.has_highlighting() {
            continue;
        }

        let highlights = parser_state.parse_and_highlight(
            &req.source,
            req.language,
            req.document_id,
            req.revision,
        );

        let _ = msg_tx.send(Msg::Syntax(SyntaxMsg::ParseCompleted {
            document_id: req.document_id,
            revision: req.revision,
            highlights,
        }));
    }
}
```

That alone should make all documents (Rust, Markdown, YAML) get initial syntax highlighting when opened via CLI.

**Optional refinement (if you really want “latest wins”)**

If you want to keep a “collapse obsolete work” behavior, do it **per document**, not globally:

- Maintain a small `HashMap<DocumentId, SyntaxParseRequest>` that stores the *latest* pending request per document.
- When you drain the channel, update the map entry for that `document_id`.
- After draining, process each entry in the map.

But for now, the simple sequential loop is safest and probably good enough.

Effort: S (≤1h), and high confidence this fixes issue (1).

---

### B. Fix “editing makes highlighting wonky” (column/offset bugs)

There are three interacting issues:

1. **Tree-sitter `column` is a byte offset; you treat it as a character index.**
   - In `parser.rs`:

     ```rust
     let node = capture.node;
     let start = node.start_position();
     let end = node.end_position();
     // ...
     line_highlights.tokens.push(HighlightToken {
         start_col: start.column,
         end_col: end.column,
         highlight: highlight_id,
     });
     ```

   - `start.column` / `end.column` are byte offsets from the start of the line.
   - Later, in `TextPainter::draw_with_highlights`, you iterate over `text.chars().enumerate()` (character indices) and compare `col` against `token.start_col` / `end_col`. For non‑ASCII characters, multibyte sequences, etc., this is wrong and leads to shifted highlights.

2. **You don’t account for tabs in highlight positions.**
   - Rendering text uses `expand_tabs_for_display` and `char_col_to_visual_col` for selections/cursors, but highlight tokens are just `start_col`/`end_col` in “logical columns”, then you only subtract `viewport.left_column`. You never remap from char columns to visual columns when tabs expand to multiple spaces.

3. **Old highlights remain visible during debounce.**
   - On edit, `Document.push_edit` increments `revision` but keeps the previous `syntax_highlights` around until the new parse completes and passes the revision check.
   - During that 50ms debounce + parse time, the tokens refer to an older revision, so the text and highlights don’t match (especially obvious while typing).

Together, that looks exactly like “highlighting becomes incorrect/misaligned after edits”.

#### B1. Normalize highlight columns to character indices

Fix this at **parse time**, so the rest of the pipeline can treat `start_col`/`end_col` as *character* columns.

In `parser.rs::extract_highlights`, pre-split the source into lines and convert byte columns to char columns:

```rust
fn extract_highlights(
    &self,
    source: &str,
    tree: &Tree,
    language: LanguageId,
    revision: u64,
) -> SyntaxHighlights {
    let query = match self.queries.get(&language) {
        Some(q) => q,
        None => return SyntaxHighlights::new(language, revision),
    };

    let mut highlights = SyntaxHighlights::new(language, revision);
    let mut cursor = QueryCursor::new();
    let source_bytes = source.as_bytes();

    // Pre-split into lines once
    let lines: Vec<&str> = source.lines().collect();

    // Helper: byte -> char column on a given line
    fn byte_to_char_col(line: &str, byte_col: usize) -> usize {
        let byte_col = byte_col.min(line.len());
        line[..byte_col].chars().count()
    }

    let mut captures = cursor.captures(query, tree.root_node(), source_bytes);
    while let Some((query_match, capture_idx)) = captures.next() {
        let capture = &query_match.captures[*capture_idx];
        let capture_name = &query.capture_names()[capture.index as usize];

        let highlight_id = match highlight_id_for_name(capture_name) {
            Some(id) => id,
            None => continue,
        };

        let node = capture.node;
        let start = node.start_position();
        let end = node.end_position();

        if start.row == end.row {
            let row = start.row as usize;
            let line = lines.get(row).copied().unwrap_or("");
            let start_char = byte_to_char_col(line, start.column as usize);
            let end_char = byte_to_char_col(line, end.column as usize);

            if start_char < end_char {
                let line_highlights = highlights.lines.entry(row).or_default();
                line_highlights.tokens.push(HighlightToken {
                    start_col: start_char,
                    end_col: end_char,
                    highlight: highlight_id,
                });
            }
        } else {
            // Multi-line node
            for row in start.row..=end.row {
                let row_usize = row as usize;
                let line = lines.get(row_usize).copied().unwrap_or("");
                let line_char_len = line.chars().count();

                let (start_char, end_char) = if row == start.row {
                    let start_char = byte_to_char_col(line, start.column as usize);
                    (start_char, line_char_len)
                } else if row == end.row {
                    let end_char = byte_to_char_col(line, end.column as usize);
                    (0, end_char)
                } else {
                    (0, line_char_len)
                };

                if start_char < end_char {
                    let line_highlights = highlights.lines.entry(row_usize).or_default();
                    line_highlights.tokens.push(HighlightToken {
                        start_col: start_char,
                        end_col: end_char,
                        highlight: highlight_id,
                    });
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // Sort tokens within each line by start column
    for line_highlights in highlights.lines.values_mut() {
        line_highlights.tokens.sort_by_key(|t| (t.start_col, t.end_col));
    }

    highlights
}
```

After this change:

- `SyntaxHighlights` stores `start_col`/`end_col` as **character columns**, consistent with how `TextPainter` iterates characters.
- You’ll no longer have byte/char mismatches causing offsets to drift, especially after editing.

Effort: M (1–3h) including tests.

#### B2. Deal with tabs and horizontal scrolling (incremental fix)

You already note this in a comment in `render_text_area`:

```rust
// Adjust token columns for horizontal scroll and tab expansion
// For now, we pass the tokens directly; a more robust implementation
// would adjust for viewport.left_column and tab expansion
let adjusted_tokens: Vec<token::syntax::HighlightToken> = line_tokens
    .iter()
    .filter_map(|t| {
        let start = t.start_col.saturating_sub(editor.viewport.left_column);
        let end = t.end_col.saturating_sub(editor.viewport.left_column);
        ...
    })
    .collect();
```

This code:

- Assumes `viewport.left_column` is measured in the same units as `start_col` (logical characters).
- But rendering uses `expand_tabs_for_display` and `viewport.left_column` is in **visual columns** (after tab expansion).
- So for lines with tabs to the left of the viewport, highlights appear shifted.

Simple step that improves correctness without rewriting everything:

- Keep `SyntaxHighlights` in character columns (from B1).
- When you adjust for scrolling, convert token char columns to **visual** columns using the same helper as for cursors/selections.

E.g. in `render_text_area`:

```rust
let line_text = document.get_line(doc_line).unwrap_or_default();
let line_text_trimmed = if line_text.ends_with('\n') {
    &line_text[..line_text.len() - 1]
} else {
    &line_text
};

let max_chars = ((rect_w as f32 - text_start_x_offset as f32) / char_width).floor() as usize;

let adjusted_tokens: Vec<token::syntax::HighlightToken> = line_tokens
    .iter()
    .filter_map(|t| {
        // Map char columns → visual columns
        let visual_start = char_col_to_visual_col(line_text_trimmed, t.start_col);
        let visual_end = char_col_to_visual_col(line_text_trimmed, t.end_col);

        let start = visual_start.saturating_sub(editor.viewport.left_column);
        let end = visual_end.saturating_sub(editor.viewport.left_column);

        if end > 0 && start < max_chars {
            Some(token::syntax::HighlightToken {
                start_col: start,
                end_col: end.min(max_chars),
                highlight: t.highlight,
            })
        } else {
            None
        }
    })
    .collect();
```

Now `draw_with_highlights` still runs over `display_text.chars().enumerate()` but `start_col`/`end_col` are in **visual columns**, consistent with how the substring is sliced (we’re skipping `viewport.left_column` visual columns from `expanded_text`).

Effort: M (1–3h), but can be done incrementally after B1.

#### B3. Clear stale highlights on edit (optional but helpful)

To avoid obviously stale/misaligned highlights during the debounce window:

In `Document::push_edit`:

```rust
pub fn push_edit(&mut self, op: EditOperation) {
    self.undo_stack.push(op);
    self.redo_stack.clear();
    self.is_modified = true;
    self.revision = self.revision.wrapping_add(1);

    // Invalidate syntax highlights; they'll be recomputed asynchronously
    self.syntax_highlights = None;
}
```

That way:

- As soon as you edit, the document is rendered without highlights instead of with *incorrect* ones.
- After debounce + parse, `ParseCompleted` installs new `syntax_highlights` at the matching revision.

Effort: S (<1h).

---

3) Rationale and trade-offs

- Fixing the **worker queue draining** is mandatory; nothing else will make multiple documents highlight correctly.
- Normalizing columns to **character units** and then to **visual units** matches the rest of your coordinate system (ropey + `char_col_to_visual_col`) and removes entire classes of “wonky” behavior.
- Clearing highlights on edit is a UX choice: brief loss of color vs briefly wrong colors. Given the async nature, “no color until up-to-date” is usually less confusing.

Alternatives (e.g., keeping global “latest wins” with a smarter dedupe, or per-document workers) are unnecessary complexity right now.

---

4) Risks and guardrails

- Changing column semantics in `SyntaxHighlights` is invasive: make sure all uses (currently just rendering functions) are updated consistently.
- Converting byte→char columns uses `.chars().count()` which is O(n) per conversion; for large lines with many captures, this could become noticeable. If it does:
  - Precompute a byte→char lookup table per line once and reuse.
- Removing the drain loop means the worker will parse more often under heavy typing. Monitor parse latency; if it becomes an issue, consider the per-document dedupe variant.

---

5) When to consider the advanced path

Revisit the design if you see:

- High CPU usage from frequent reparsing on very large files.
- Many documents open and active at once, with noticeable worker lag.
- Need for partial/incremental updates instead of full parses on each debounce.

Then you’d want:

- Real incremental parsing (`Tree::edit` + `Parser::parse(new_text, Some(&old_tree))`).
- Per-document queues or a small worker pool.
- Smarter invalidation of only affected lines’ highlights.

---

6) Optional advanced path (outline)

- Change `SyntaxHighlights` to be per‑line and lazily updated, with an invalidation API:
  - `invalidate_range(start_line, end_line)`.
- Use tree-sitter incremental edits to keep trees in sync with buffer changes.
- For each edit, compute affected line range, requery only that region in the tree, and update highlights for those lines.
- Maintain a per-document request coalescing layer so worker always processes “latest edits for doc X” without dropping other docs’ work.

For now, implementing A + B1 (+ optionally B2/B3) will fix the two reported issues with minimal complexity.
```

## Assistant

Done! Here's a summary of all the fixes and additions:

### Fixes Applied

#### Bug 1: Only first document gets syntax highlighting
- **Cause**: The worker loop was draining all queued requests and only processing the last one globally, regardless of which document it was for
- **Fix**: Changed [src/runtime/app.rs](file://~/code/rust-editor/src/runtime/app.rs#L925-L962) to use per-document deduplication with a HashMap, so each document's latest request is processed

#### Bug 2: Editing makes highlighting wonky
- **Cause**: Tree-sitter returns byte offsets but we were treating them as character indices
- **Fix**: Added `byte_to_char_col()` helper in [src/syntax/parser.rs](file://~/code/rust-editor/src/syntax/parser.rs#L141-L145) to convert byte columns to character columns

#### Bug 3: Stale highlights during edits
- **Fix**: Modified [src/model/document.rs](file://~/code/rust-editor/src/model/document.rs#L217-L219) to clear `syntax_highlights` in `push_edit()` so we don't show misaligned highlights during the debounce window

### New Features

#### HTML, CSS, JavaScript Support
- Added tree-sitter dependencies for HTML, CSS, and JavaScript
- Added `LanguageId::Html`, `LanguageId::Css`, `LanguageId::JavaScript` variants
- Created highlight queries:
  - [queries/html/highlights.scm](file://~/code/rust-editor/queries/html/highlights.scm)
  - [queries/css/highlights.scm](file://~/code/rust-editor/queries/css/highlights.scm)
  - [queries/javascript/highlights.scm](file://~/code/rust-editor/queries/javascript/highlights.scm)
- Created [samples/sample.html](file://~/code/rust-editor/samples/sample.html) with embedded CSS and JavaScript

Run `make dev` to test with all 4 files including the new HTML sample!
